And I’ll be looking right at her while she's speaking. But a minute later, I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she just said. It's just been happening a bit too often lately, so I figured I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you all the time when your wife is talking to you, or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not all the time. I'd say just sometimes. I mean, it's not like I miss every single word she ever says to me. It just... it happens often enough that we both notice it, and she gets a bit frustrated. But definitely not every time we talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember what your wife said if someone asked you to recall it right after she finished talking?\n\nPatient: Honestly, probably not. Like I mentioned a minute ago about my cousin's appointments and rides, I can be looking right at her the whole time she's speaking. But even just a minute later, I realize I didn't actually take in half of what she just said. So if you asked me to repeat it back right after she finished, I'd probably still be missing a good chunk of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you when you're reading something, like a book or an article, or is it mainly when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: It happens with reading and other things, too, yeah. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order, or if I'm listening in on a parish planning call... I usually start off fine. But before long, my mind just wanders off. I'll realize I haven't been paying attention, and I have to go back over the same notes or the same paragraph like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty paying attention and remembering what was said or read caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. It's started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she just can’t count on me with my cousin’s care right now, which is tough. And with the catering stuff... I’ve had to start double-checking myself a lot, or even redoing tasks, just to make sure the small jobs don’t get mixed up. So, it's definitely making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don't feel like I have to be moving around or anything like that. I might tap my foot sometimes without thinking about it, but I wouldn't say I feel restless. It's more just my mind wandering off than me needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you realize you've missed something that was said to you or that you've read?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel anxious, no. Um, it's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I get a bit annoyed with myself when I realize I spaced out again, especially if my wife is the one talking to me. But I don't feel stressed out or panicked about it. It's really just a hassle having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to reread the same page over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm usually pretty quiet, actually. I don't really have a problem waiting for someone to finish talking. If anything, I'm probably just spacing out while they're talking, not trying to talk over them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things on impulse, without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really an impulsive guy. I tend to take my time with things, mostly. So, no, jumping into things without thinking isn't really an issue for me. Like I said, it's really just the focus and the spacing out that gives me trouble.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, I guess what really brought me in is... my wife will be talking to me, telling me about things like my cousin’s appointments or rides he needs. And I’ll be looking right at her while she's speaking. But a minute later, I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she just said. It's just been happening a bit too often lately, so I figured I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you all the time when your wife is talking to you, or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not all the time. I'd say just sometimes. I mean, it's not like I miss every single word she ever says to me. It just... it happens often enough that we both notice it, and she gets a bit frustrated. But definitely not every time we talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember what your wife said if someone asked you to recall it right after she finished talking?\n\nPatient: Honestly, probably not. Like I mentioned a minute ago about my cousin's appointments and rides, I can be looking right at her the whole time she's speaking. But even just a minute later, I realize I didn't actually take in half of what she just said. So if you asked me to repeat it back right after she finished, I'd probably still be missing a good chunk of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you when you're reading something, like a book or an article, or is it mainly when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: It happens with reading and other things, too, yeah. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order, or if I'm listening in on a parish planning call... I usually start off fine. But before long, my mind just wanders off. I'll realize I haven't been paying attention, and I have to go back over the same notes or the same paragraph like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty paying attention and remembering what was said or read caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. It's started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she just can’t count on me with my cousin’s care right now, which is tough. And with the catering stuff... I’ve had to start double-checking myself a lot, or even redoing tasks, just to make sure the small jobs don’t get mixed up. So, it's definitely making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don't feel like I have to be moving around or anything like that. I might tap my foot sometimes without thinking about it, but I wouldn't say I feel restless. It's more just my mind wandering off than me needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you realize you've missed something that was said to you or that you've read?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel anxious, no. Um, it's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I get a bit annoyed with myself when I realize I spaced out again, especially if my wife is the one talking to me. But I don't feel stressed out or panicked about it. It's really just a hassle having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to reread the same page over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm usually pretty quiet, actually. I don't really have a problem waiting for someone to finish talking. If anything, I'm probably just spacing out while they're talking, not trying to talk over them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things on impulse, without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really an impulsive guy. I tend to take my time with things, mostly. So, no, jumping into things without thinking isn't really an issue for me. Like I said, it's really just the focus and the spacing out that gives me trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Um, I can sit still just fine. I don't really get restless or feel the need to move around a lot. Like I was saying, I'm a pretty quiet guy, so... no, fidgeting isn't really something I deal with. It's really just my mind wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty engaging in activities quietly?\n\nPatient: No, no trouble with that at all. I actually prefer quiet activities, to be honest. If I'm reading or just doing something around the house, I'm perfectly fine being quiet. So, yeah, making too much noise or being loud definitely isn't an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or overwhelmed when you realize you've missed something important due to your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Um, not really, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. And I’ll be looking right at her while she's speaking. But a minute later, I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she just said. It's just been happening a bit too often lately, so I figured I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you all the time when your wife is talking to you, or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not all the time. I'd say just sometimes. I mean, it's not like I miss every single word she ever says to me. It just... it happens often enough that we both notice it, and she gets a bit frustrated. But definitely not every time we talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember what your wife said if someone asked you to recall it right after she finished talking?\n\nPatient: Honestly, probably not. Like I mentioned a minute ago about my cousin's appointments and rides, I can be looking right at her the whole time she's speaking. But even just a minute later, I realize I didn't actually take in half of what she just said. So if you asked me to repeat it back right after she finished, I'd probably still be missing a good chunk of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you when you're reading something, like a book or an article, or is it mainly when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: It happens with reading and other things, too, yeah. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order, or if I'm listening in on a parish planning call... I usually start off fine. But before long, my mind just wanders off. I'll realize I haven't been paying attention, and I have to go back over the same notes or the same paragraph like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty paying attention and remembering what was said or read caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. It's started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she just can’t count on me with my cousin’s care right now, which is tough. And with the catering stuff... I’ve had to start double-checking myself a lot, or even redoing tasks, just to make sure the small jobs don’t get mixed up. So, it's definitely making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don't feel like I have to be moving around or anything like that. I might tap my foot sometimes without thinking about it, but I wouldn't say I feel restless. It's more just my mind wandering off than me needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you realize you've missed something that was said to you or that you've read?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel anxious, no. Um, it's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I get a bit annoyed with myself when I realize I spaced out again, especially if my wife is the one talking to me. It's really just a hassle having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to reread the same page over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Well, I guess what really brought me in is... my wife will be talking to me, telling me about things like my cousin’s appointments or rides he needs. And I’ll be looking right at her while she's speaking. But a minute later, I realize I didn’t actually take in half of what she just said. It's just been happening a bit too often lately, so I figured I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you all the time when your wife is talking to you, or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not all the time. I'd say just sometimes. I mean, it's not like I miss every single word she ever says to me. It just... it happens often enough that we both notice it, and she gets a bit frustrated. But definitely not every time we talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember what your wife said if someone asked you to recall it right after she finished talking?\n\nPatient: Honestly, probably not. Like I mentioned a minute ago about my cousin's appointments and rides, I can be looking right at her the whole time she's speaking. But even just a minute later, I realize I didn't actually take in half of what she just said. So if you asked me to repeat it back right after she finished, I'd probably still be missing a good chunk of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you when you're reading something, like a book or an article, or is it mainly when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: It happens with reading and other things, too, yeah. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order, or if I'm listening in on a parish planning call... I usually start off fine. But before long, my mind just wanders off. I'll realize I haven't been paying attention, and I have to go back over the same notes or the same paragraph like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty paying attention and remembering what was said or read caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. It's started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she just can’t count on me with my cousin’s care right now, which is tough. And with the catering stuff... I’ve had to start double-checking myself a lot, or even redoing tasks, just to make sure the small jobs don’t get mixed up. So, it's definitely making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don't feel like I have to be moving around or anything like that. I might tap my foot sometimes without thinking about it, but I wouldn't say I feel restless. It's more just my mind wandering off than me needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you realize you've missed something that was said to you or that you've read?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel anxious, no. Um, it's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I get a bit annoyed with myself when I realize I spaced out again, especially if my wife is the one talking to me. But I don't feel stressed out or panicked about it. It's really just a hassle having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to reread the same page over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm usually pretty quiet, actually. I don't really have a problem waiting for someone to finish talking. If anything, I'm probably just spacing out while they're talking, not trying to talk over them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things on impulse, without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really an impulsive guy. I tend to take my time with things, mostly. So, no, jumping into things without thinking isn't really an issue for me. Like I said, it's really just the focus and the spacing out that gives me trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Um, I can sit still just fine. I don't really get restless or feel the need to move around a lot. Like I was saying, I'm a pretty quiet guy, so... no, fidgeting isn't really something I deal with. It's really just my mind wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty engaging in activities quietly?\n\nPatient: No, no trouble with that at all. I actually prefer quiet activities, to be honest. If I'm reading or just doing something around the house, I'm perfectly fine being quiet. So, yeah, making too much noise or being loud definitely isn't an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or overwhelmed when you realize you've missed something important due to your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Um, not really, no. I mean, it's a little annoying when it happens, sure, but I wouldn't say I get upset or overwhelmed. Since I'm not working these days, there usually aren't any big emergencies if I miss a detail here or there. I just kind of... ask someone to repeat themselves or try to catch up later. So, no, it doesn't really bother me on that level.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind wandering and missing details is affecting your mood?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it affects my mood. Um, I'm generally in a pretty good mood most days. Like I mentioned, it's maybe just a tiny bit annoying when I lose track of a conversation, but... no, it doesn't make me sad or irritable or anything like that. My mood stays pretty steady.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with paying attention and remembering what was said or read?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's been about 11 months. Um, it became noticeably harder right around the time my cousin moved in with us after having surgery.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or relationships beyond the frustration and hassle you've mentioned, due to your difficulty with paying attention and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I suppose it has a bit. It’s actually started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she can’t really count on me when it comes to helping with my cousin’s care. And, um, even though I'm not working a regular job right now, I do take on some catering tasks, and I’ve had to constantly double-check or even redo things just so those small jobs don’t get mixed up. So it definitely has an effect on my day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this difficulty with paying attention happening in other areas of your life besides conversations with your wife and work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, let me think... no, it's pretty much just those two main areas. Like I said, it's mostly the stuff at home with my wife and helping out with my cousin, and then those catering gigs. I haven't really noticed it being an issue anywhere else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's just been happening a bit too often lately, so I figured I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you all the time when your wife is talking to you, or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not all the time. I'd say just sometimes. I mean, it's not like I miss every single word she ever says to me. It just... it happens often enough that we both notice it, and she gets a bit frustrated. But definitely not every time we talk.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would remember what your wife said if someone asked you to recall it right after she finished talking?\n\nPatient: Honestly, probably not. Like I mentioned a minute ago about my cousin's appointments and rides, I can be looking right at her the whole time she's speaking. But even just a minute later, I realize I didn't actually take in half of what she just said. So if you asked me to repeat it back right after she finished, I'd probably still be missing a good chunk of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you when you're reading something, like a book or an article, or is it mainly when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: It happens with reading and other things, too, yeah. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order, or if I'm listening in on a parish planning call... I usually start off fine. But before long, my mind just wanders off. I'll realize I haven't been paying attention, and I have to go back over the same notes or the same paragraph like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty paying attention and remembering what was said or read caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. It's started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she just can’t count on me with my cousin’s care right now, which is tough. And with the catering stuff... I’ve had to start double-checking myself a lot, or even redoing tasks, just to make sure the small jobs don’t get mixed up. So, it's definitely making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don't feel like I have to be moving around or anything like that. I might tap my foot sometimes without thinking about it, but I wouldn't say I feel restless. It's more just my mind wandering off than me needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you realize you've missed something that was said to you or that you've read?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel anxious, no. Um, it's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I get a bit annoyed with myself when I realize I spaced out again, especially if my wife is the one talking to me. But I don't feel stressed out or panicked about it. It's really just a hassle having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to reread the same page over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'd say just sometimes. I mean, it's not like I miss every single word she ever says to me. It just... it happens often enough that we both notice it, and she gets a bit frustrated. Like I mentioned a minute ago about my cousin's appointments and rides, I can be looking right at her the whole time she's speaking. But even just a minute later, I realize I didn't actually take in half of what she just said. So if you asked me to repeat it back right after she finished, I'd probably still be missing a good chunk of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you when you're reading something, like a book or an article, or is it mainly when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: It happens with reading and other things, too, yeah. Like, if I sit down to work out a catering order, or if I'm listening in on a parish planning call... I usually start off fine. But before long, my mind just wanders off. I'll realize I haven't been paying attention, and I have to go back over the same notes or the same paragraph like I never even read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Has this difficulty paying attention and remembering what was said or read caused any problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. It's started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she just can’t count on me with my cousin’s care right now, which is tough. And with the catering stuff... I’ve had to start double-checking myself a lot, or even redoing tasks, just to make sure the small jobs don’t get mixed up. So, it's definitely making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don't feel like I have to be moving around or anything like that. I might tap my foot sometimes without thinking about it, but I wouldn't say I feel restless. It's more just my mind wandering off than me needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you realize you've missed something that was said to you or that you've read?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel anxious, no. Um, it's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I get a bit annoyed with myself when I realize I spaced out again, especially if my wife is the one talking to me. But I don't feel stressed out or panicked about it. Like I said, it's really just the focus and the spacing out that gives me trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Um, I can sit still just fine. I don't really get restless or feel the need to move around a lot. Like I was saying, I'm a pretty quiet guy, so... no, fidgeting isn't really something I deal with. It's really just my mind wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty engaging in activities quietly?\n\nPatient: No, no trouble with that at all. My mood stays pretty steady.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with paying attention and remembering what was said or read?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's been about 11 months. So it definitely has an effect on my day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this difficulty with paying attention happening in other areas of your life besides conversations with your wife and work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. So, it's definitely making things harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I don't feel like I have to be moving around or anything like that. I might tap my foot sometimes without thinking about it, but I wouldn't say I feel restless. It's more just my mind wandering off than me needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you realize you've missed something that was said to you or that you've read?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say I feel anxious, no. Um, it's more just... frustrating, I guess? Like, I get a bit annoyed with myself when I realize I spaced out again, especially if my wife is the one talking to me. But I don't feel stressed out or panicked about it. It's really just a hassle having to ask people to repeat themselves or having to reread the same page over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm usually pretty quiet, actually. I don't really have a problem waiting for someone to finish talking. If anything, I'm probably just spacing out while they're talking, not trying to talk over them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself doing things on impulse, without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really an impulsive guy. I tend to take my time with things, mostly. So, no, jumping into things without thinking isn't really an issue for me. Like I said, it's really just the focus and the spacing out that gives me trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Um, I can sit still just fine. I don't really get restless or feel the need to move around a lot. Like I was saying, I'm a pretty quiet guy, so... no, fidgeting isn't really something I deal with. It's really just my mind wandering off, not my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty engaging in activities quietly?\n\nPatient: No, no trouble with that at all. I actually prefer quiet activities, to be honest. If I'm reading or just doing something around the house, I'm perfectly fine being quiet. So, yeah, making too much noise or being loud definitely isn't an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or overwhelmed when you realize you've missed something important due to your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Um, not really, no. I mean, it's a little annoying when it happens, sure, but I wouldn't say I get upset or overwhelmed. Since I'm not working these days, there usually aren't any big emergencies if I miss a detail here or there. I just kind of... ask someone to repeat themselves or try to catch up later. So, no, it doesn't really bother me on that level.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mind wandering and missing details is affecting your mood?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it affects my mood. Um, I'm generally in a pretty good mood most days. Like I mentioned, it's maybe just a tiny bit annoying when I lose track of a conversation, but... no, it doesn't make me sad or irritable or anything like that. My mood stays pretty steady.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with paying attention and remembering what was said or read?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's been about 11 months. Um, it became noticeably harder right around the time my cousin moved in with us after having surgery.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or relationships beyond the frustration and hassle you've mentioned, due to your difficulty with paying attention and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I suppose it has a bit. It’s actually started causing some tension at home, to be honest. My wife feels like she can’t really count on me when it comes to helping with my cousin’s care. And, um, even though I'm not working a regular job right now, I do take on some catering tasks, and I’ve had to constantly double-check or even redo things just so those small jobs don’t get mixed up. So it definitely has an effect on my day-to-day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this difficulty with paying attention happening in other areas of your life besides conversations with your wife and work-related tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Um, let me think... no, it's pretty much just those two main areas. Like I said, it's mostly the stuff at home with my wife and helping out with my cousin, and then those catering gigs. I haven't really noticed it being an issue anywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty paying attention is triggered by or happens more often when you're feeling stressed or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, it happens whether I'm having a good day or a bad day. It doesn't seem to matter if I'm feeling stressed out or perfectly fine, the trouble focusing is just kind of... there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant life changes or stressors in the past year, aside from your cousin moving in?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Aside from my cousin coming to live with us, things have been pretty much the same. I haven't had any other major changes or big stressors like that over the past year. Everything else has been pretty routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant emotional reactions, such as depression or anxiety, in response to your cousin moving in with you after surgery?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I mean, it's an adjustment, sure, having someone else in the house, but I haven't felt depressed or anxious about it. It's just something we're doing to help him out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you're outside of your body watching yourself, or like you're in a fog and things aren't real?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've ever felt anything like that. I've never felt like I was outside my body or that things weren't real.
